# **CAMERA V1 — GOVERNANCE DOCTRINE**

**Document Status:** MASTER GOVERNANCE DOCUMENT  
 **Version:** V1.0  
 **Product:** CAMERA  
 **Purpose:** Control the development of the CAMERA book, educational course, workbook, presentation materials, instructor materials, supporting resources, and future revisions.

---

# **1\. PURPOSE OF THIS DOCUMENT**

This document establishes the governing rules for the development of CAMERA V1.

CAMERA is being developed as an educational system that teaches businesses and individuals how to understand, plan, create, produce, present, edit, distribute, and improve their own marketing and authority content using practical media skills and modern tools, including AI.

The purpose of this Governance Doctrine is to ensure that CAMERA remains coherent as the product is developed.

It exists specifically to prevent:

* curriculum drift  
* unnecessary redesign  
* accidental expansion of scope  
* duplication  
* contradictory teaching  
* conversion of the course into a generic marketing course  
* conversion of the course into an AI course  
* premature incorporation of ATLAS or LEGAiSEE  
* rebuilding material that already exists  
* allowing individual lessons or presentations to redefine the overall system

The Master TOC established under this doctrine is the controlling architecture for CAMERA V1.

---

# **2\. THE CAMERA PRODUCT**

CAMERA is a practical media education system.

It teaches people how to become capable of producing their own content rather than remaining completely dependent upon outside production companies, agencies, freelancers, or increasingly complicated technology stacks.

The course uses the instructor’s professional media experience to teach the practical realities of:

* presenting on camera  
* looking better on camera  
* sounding better on camera  
* communicating authority  
* understanding cameras and phones  
* framing  
* composition  
* lighting  
* audio  
* backgrounds  
* equipment selection  
* software selection  
* recording  
* editing  
* scripting  
* content structure  
* publishing  
* platform considerations  
* repurposing  
* workflow  
* consistency  
* modern AI-assisted content workflows

CAMERA is fundamentally about **teaching the participant how to do it themselves.**

The instructor is the teacher, advisor, demonstrator, and coach.

CAMERA is not primarily a production service.

---

# **3\. THE CENTRAL EDUCATIONAL PROMISE**

The fundamental promise of CAMERA is:

**You do not have to become a professional filmmaker to produce professional-looking, useful, credible marketing content for your own business.**

Participants should leave the program substantially more capable of determining:

* what they need  
* what they do not need  
* what equipment is appropriate  
* what software is appropriate  
* how to set up a recording environment  
* how to present themselves  
* how to structure content  
* how to record it  
* how to edit it  
* how to use AI appropriately  
* how to turn individual pieces of content into an ongoing system

The course should reduce unnecessary complexity rather than create it.

---

# **4\. CAMERA IS NOT A ONE-SIZE-FITS-ALL PRODUCTION MODEL**

CAMERA must accommodate different types of participants.

Examples include, but are not limited to:

* a traditional small-business owner  
* a professional services firm  
* a medical practice  
* a law firm  
* an automotive business  
* a retail business  
* a restaurant or hospitality business  
* a founder-led company  
* a corporate communications team  
* a designated company spokesperson  
* a sales team  
* an employee content team  
* a professional expert  
* a consultant  
* a coach  
* a personal brand  
* an influencer  
* an individual creator

These are examples of applications.

They are not restrictions on the CAMERA market.

The curriculum must therefore teach **principles that can be adapted to different businesses and individuals.**

---

# **5\. CAMERA MUST TEACH THE BUSINESS CONTEXT**

CAMERA is not merely a course about cameras.

The participant must understand why they are creating content before spending time or money producing it.

The curriculum therefore must connect media execution to:

**Business → Marketing → Content → Production → Distribution → Response → Improvement**

Technical instruction exists to serve the communication objective.

A technically excellent video that communicates nothing useful is not a successful CAMERA outcome.

---

# **6\. CAMERA AND THE CAMERA AUTHORITY SYSTEM**

The existing CAMERA framework establishes six interlocking dimensions:

**C — Character Positioning**

**A — Audience Targeting**

**M — Monetization Design**

**E — Engagement Engineering**

**R — Repurposing Infrastructure**

**A — AI Acceleration**

These concepts form part of the CAMERA intellectual framework and must be preserved during curriculum development.

The Master TOC will determine how these concepts are taught and where practical media instruction fits within the larger educational sequence.

The acronym must not be casually redefined during V1 development.

If a potential improvement would require changing one of the six dimensions, it belongs in the V2 change-control process unless the change is necessary to correct an actual error in the existing system.

---

# **7\. CAMERA, ATLAS, AND LEGAiSEE ARE SEPARATE PRODUCTS**

This separation is mandatory.

## **CAMERA**

CAMERA teaches media, content creation, presentation, production, workflow, and appropriate use of modern tools.

## **ATLAS**

ATLAS is a separate strategic authority/content education system.

ATLAS may eventually expand upon concepts introduced through CAMERA, but ATLAS must not be allowed to silently become part of CAMERA V1.

## **LEGAiSEE**

LEGAiSEE is **Business Archaeology.**

Its function is to excavate buried historical business and marketing artifacts, identify valuable material that has been forgotten or abandoned, and reform that material for the modern market.

LEGAiSEE is separate from CAMERA and ATLAS.

CAMERA may eventually be used as an implementation capability following other strategic work, but CAMERA V1 must not become a LEGAiSEE course.

---

# **8\. AI’S ROLE IN CAMERA**

AI is part of the modern media environment and therefore belongs in CAMERA.

However:

**CAMERA is not an AI course.**

AI is taught as a tool within the participant’s existing marketing and content workflow.

Appropriate applications may include:

* ideation  
* research assistance  
* outlining  
* scripting  
* script refinement  
* headline development  
* hook development  
* content variations  
* transcription  
* caption generation  
* repurposing  
* editing assistance  
* organization  
* workflow assistance  
* analysis  
* iteration

The curriculum should also teach participants to understand the limitations of AI.

The objective is not to encourage participants to subscribe to ten different AI platforms.

The objective is to teach them how to use appropriate AI tools effectively without allowing the tools to replace their judgment, experience, personality, expertise, or responsibility for the final content.

---

# **9\. THE BOOK AND THE COURSE**

CAMERA will contain multiple educational products derived from the same intellectual system.

They must not be treated as identical.

## **The Book**

The book is the comprehensive study and reference resource.

It can contain more explanation, detail, background, examples, supporting information, and reference material than can reasonably be delivered during a live session.

## **The Live Course**

The live course is the teaching experience.

It must:

* explain concepts  
* demonstrate concepts  
* provide examples  
* allow questions  
* conduct exercises  
* allow participants to apply concepts  
* provide instructor guidance  
* move participants toward implementation

The instructor must never simply read the book to participants.

## **The Workbook**

The workbook is the participant’s working document.

It converts concepts into:

* questions  
* exercises  
* decisions  
* plans  
* checklists  
* implementation tasks  
* self-evaluation

## **The Slide Deck**

The slide deck is client-facing teaching support.

It is not the book.

It is not the instructor script.

It is not a collection of every point the instructor intends to make.

Slides should visually and intellectually support the instructor’s teaching.

## **The Instructor Prompter**

The instructor script is private.

It contains the actual teaching language, explanations, examples, transitions, questions, demonstrations, and timing required to deliver the lesson.

---

# **10\. LIVE COURSE DEPTH STANDARD**

CAMERA is an educational course, not a webinar.

A live session must provide substantial instruction.

The target format may be approximately 60–120 minutes depending upon the instructional requirements of the material.

The duration is subordinate to educational completeness.

The curriculum must never be artificially compressed merely to satisfy a predetermined runtime.

If a subject requires two hours to teach properly, the session may be two hours.

If a particular lesson can be taught properly in less time, it should not be padded merely to increase runtime.

**Teaching quality determines duration.**

---

# **11\. REQUIRED STRUCTURE OF LIVE TEACHING**

Where appropriate, lessons should follow a structure similar to:

**Concept**

→ Explain what it is.

**Why It Matters**

→ Explain why the participant should care.

**Demonstration / Example**

→ Show how the concept appears in real use.

**Application**

→ Have the participant apply it to their own situation.

**Discussion / Questions**

→ Resolve confusion and reinforce understanding.

**Implementation**

→ Establish what the participant will actually do afterward.

Not every lesson requires every element, but the live course must consistently move beyond information delivery into understanding and application.

---

# **12\. CLIENT-FACING SLIDE STANDARD**

Slides must support teaching rather than compete with it.

A slide may contain:

* a concept  
* a diagram  
* a comparison  
* a photograph  
* a visual example  
* a process  
* a short statement  
* a framework  
* a checklist  
* an exercise prompt  
* a demonstration setup  
* a key takeaway

Slides should not routinely become pages of dense instructional prose.

The instructor is responsible for the explanation.

---

# **13\. INSTRUCTOR SCRIPT STANDARD**

The instructor script must contain enough actual language to function as a presenter prompter.

It must not consist merely of:

* bullet points  
* keywords  
* topic reminders  
* vague notes such as “talk about lighting”

Instead, the instructor should be able to open the script during a live session and teach from it.

The script should include:

* actual teaching language  
* explanations  
* examples  
* transitions  
* questions  
* demonstrations  
* exercise instructions  
* timing guidance  
* reminders where necessary

The instructor may naturally deviate from the script during live discussion.

The script is the controlled baseline, not a prison.

---

# **14\. STUDENT WORKBOOK STANDARD**

Every workbook section must have a reason for existing.

Workbook material should allow participants to actively construct something useful.

Examples include:

* identifying their audience  
* defining their message  
* selecting equipment  
* designing a recording environment  
* establishing a content workflow  
* writing a script  
* evaluating a recording  
* planning a content series  
* developing a repurposing workflow  
* creating an AI-assisted workflow

The workbook should not simply repeat the slide deck.

---

# **15\. PRACTICAL MEDIA STANDARD**

CAMERA must remain grounded in practical media production.

The course should teach participants how to make sensible decisions at different levels of resources.

The system must accommodate:

### **Level 1 — Start With What You Have**

For example:

* existing smartphone  
* existing computer  
* natural light  
* basic room  
* simple free software

### **Level 2 — Improve the Weakest Link**

Participants learn to identify whether the limiting factor is:

* audio  
* lighting  
* framing  
* environment  
* camera  
* editing  
* presentation  
* message

before spending money.

### **Level 3 — Purposeful Upgrade**

Participants learn when additional equipment or software actually produces a meaningful improvement.

### **Level 4 — Professional / Advanced Setup**

Participants who require higher production capability can be taught how to build toward it.

The course must not imply that expensive equipment automatically produces authoritative content.

---

# **16\. GEAR RECOMMENDATION GOVERNANCE**

Gear recommendations must be organized by **use case and budget**, not by an arbitrary list of products.

The curriculum should distinguish between:

* required  
* recommended  
* optional  
* advanced

Recommendations should explain **why** an item matters.

Where product examples are used, they should be treated as examples rather than permanent requirements.

Technology changes.

The underlying principles should remain useful even when specific products change.

---

# **17\. SOFTWARE GOVERNANCE**

CAMERA must avoid unnecessary software proliferation.

The course should teach participants how to select a practical software workflow.

A participant should not leave believing they need:

* multiple editing platforms  
* multiple AI subscriptions  
* multiple caption systems  
* multiple automation services  
* multiple publishing systems

unless their actual workflow requires them.

The teaching principle is:

**Choose the simplest tool that reliably accomplishes the job.**

---

# **18\. CONTENT CREATION GOVERNANCE**

CAMERA does not teach participants to produce content merely because they can.

Content should have a purpose.

The curriculum should continually connect:

**Who is this for?**

**Why would they care?**

**What does this communicate?**

**What does it accomplish?**

**What should happen next?**

Technical production is subordinate to communication.

---

# **19\. NO DRIFT RULE**

Once the Master TOC is approved, it becomes the controlling architecture.

During lesson development, an interesting new idea does not automatically become curriculum.

Every proposed addition must answer:

1. What problem does it solve?  
2. Where does it belong in the Master TOC?  
3. Is it necessary for V1?  
4. Does it duplicate existing material?  
5. Does it change the scope of CAMERA?  
6. Does it belong in ATLAS instead?  
7. Does it belong in a supporting resource instead?  
8. Is it better reserved for V2?

If the answer is unclear, the material is not automatically added.

---

# **20\. V1 CHANGE CONTROL**

Potential improvements discovered during development will be recorded in a:

**CAMERA V2 / FUTURE DEVELOPMENT REGISTER**

They will not automatically alter V1.

A V1 change should occur only when the proposed change is necessary because of:

* factual error  
* structural contradiction  
* missing material required to fulfill the existing promise  
* unusable instructional sequence  
* significant practical problem  
* legal/safety issue  
* technology change that makes existing instruction unusable

Ideas that are simply “better,” “cooler,” or “interesting” should normally be recorded for later review.

---

# **21\. SOURCE FIDELITY**

Existing CAMERA source material is the foundation.

During the Master TOC and curriculum build:

**Existing terminology, frameworks, concepts, and intended meaning must be preserved unless an explicit editorial decision changes them.**

We must distinguish between:

**Source-derived material**

and

**New material created to support teaching.**

New material may be necessary.

It must not silently replace the original intellectual structure.

---

# **22\. CURRICULUM TRACEABILITY**

Every substantive curriculum component should ultimately have a traceable location.

The hierarchy will be:

**CAMERA**

→ **Module**

→ **Chapter**

→ **Subchapter**

→ **Lesson**

→ **Teaching Material**

→ **Slide**

→ **Workbook**

→ **Assignment**

This allows every component to answer:

**Where does this belong?**

**Why is it being taught?**

**What does it support?**

**What does the participant do with it?**

---

The CAMERA V1 document system will ultimately consist of:

### **GOVERNANCE**

**CAMERA V1 Governance Doctrine**

### **ARCHITECTURE**

**CAMERA V1 Master TOC**

### **SOURCE CONTROL**

**CAMERA V1 Templates**

---

# **24\. INSTRUCTOR OPERATING MANUAL**

The Instructor Operating Manual is part of the internal CAMERA system.

It will eventually document:

* how to prepare a cohort  
* how to prepare each session  
* how to use the slides  
* how to use the prompter  
* how to conduct exercises  
* how to handle questions  
* how to review student work  
* how to provide feedback  
* how to handle different participant skill levels  
* how to manage timing  
* how to document completion  
* how to conduct post-course follow-up

The Instructor Operating Manual is **not** the student curriculum.

It is the operating system for delivering the curriculum consistently.

---

# **25\. COURSE COMPLETION STANDARD**

A participant should not receive a completion certificate merely because they attended a predetermined number of Zoom meetings.

The final completion requirements will be established in the Master TOC and Course Architecture.

They should demonstrate meaningful participation and completion of the required educational work.

The exact requirements are to be established later and must not be invented separately by individual sessions.

---

# **26\. COMMERCIAL GOVERNANCE**

The educational product and its price are separate decisions.

The current commercial concept may include:

* virtual cohort  
* virtual individual instruction  
* local in-person group instruction  
* local in-person individual instruction  
* future specialized versions

However, commercial packaging must not alter the underlying educational architecture without an explicit curriculum decision.

The same core CAMERA intellectual system should be capable of being delivered in different formats.

---

# **27\. VERSION CONTROL**

All CAMERA documents must carry:

**Product:** CAMERA  
 **Version:** V1.0  
 **Document Type:** \[specific document\]  
 **Status:** Draft / Review / Approved / Locked  
 **Last Revised:** \[date\]

Once a document is locked, changes require version control.

Example:

**CAMERA V1.0**

→ initial production version

**CAMERA V1.1**

→ corrections and minor improvements

**CAMERA V2.0**

→ substantive curriculum change

This prevents silent evolution of the system.

---

# **28\. THE MASTER TOC IS THE NEXT GOVERNING DOCUMENT**

Following approval of this Governance Doctrine, the next document to create is:

# **CAMERA V1 — MASTER TABLE OF CONTENTS**

The Master TOC must contain the **entire CAMERA system**, including:

* all modules  
* all chapters  
* all subchapters  
* all lessons  
* practical instruction  
* exercises  
* implementation components  
* supporting materials

It will be constructed from the existing CAMERA source material before the individual live sessions are written.

Once approved, the Master TOC becomes the structural authority for the remainder of the CAMERA V1 build.

---

# **29\. GOVERNING PRINCIPLE**

The CAMERA V1 build follows one fundamental rule:

**Build the system once. Teach it clearly. Document it completely. Improve it deliberately. Do not redesign it every time a new idea appears.**

**END OF CAMERA V1 GOVERNANCE DOCTRINE**

# **CAMERA™ V1 — MASTER TABLE OF CONTENTS**

**CAMERA™ Authority Media Training System**

**Purpose:** Teach businesses, business owners, founders, professionals, spokespersons, and content teams how to plan, create, present, produce, edit, publish, and sustain their own effective video and content marketing in the modern AI-enabled media environment.

**V1 Position:** Education and implementation training. CAMERA does not provide the Business Archaeology service of LEGAiSEE. Students learn how to build and operate their own content capability.

**Primary Delivery:** Live virtual instruction, with optional in-person delivery.

**Core Student Outcome:** By completion, the student should understand what their business actually needs, know how to create it themselves, know what equipment and software are appropriate for their situation, be capable of appearing professionally on camera, understand how to turn expertise into useful content, and possess a repeatable content-production workflow that can be sustained after the course.

# **CAMERA V1 — MASTER COURSE ASSET MAP**

The Master TOC above becomes the controlling structure for **every CAMERA V1 asset**.

For each teaching chapter that is included in the live course, the build will ultimately contain:

**1\. MASTER BOOK CHAPTER**  
 The complete educational treatment and permanent reference material.

**2\. STUDENT CHAPTER / HOME STUDY VERSION**  
 The appropriate chapter or chapter section assigned for review outside class.

**3\. LIVE TEACHING SCRIPT**  
 The condensed instructor-facing teaching version—not a summary and not a webinar script.

**4\. CLIENT-FACING SLIDES**  
 Visual teaching support containing the concepts, demonstrations, diagrams, examples, exercises, and reference information students need to see.

**5\. INSTRUCTOR PROMPTER**  
 Full spoken teaching language corresponding slide-by-slide with the presentation.

**6\. LIVE EXERCISES**  
 Activities students actually perform during the session.

**7\. HOMEWORK / IMPLEMENTATION ASSIGNMENT**  
 Work that moves the student’s own business forward.

**8\. WORKBOOK PAGES**  
 The corresponding student worksheets and implementation tools.

**9\. CHECKLISTS / SOPs**  
 Practical tools students can use after the course.

**10\. INSTRUCTOR NOTES**  
 Timing, demonstrations, anticipated questions, teaching cautions, and transition instructions.

**11\. ASSESSMENT**  
 The method for determining whether the student actually learned and implemented the material.

**12\. COMPLETION REQUIREMENT**  
 The measurable requirement for that section or module.

---

# **CAMERA V1 — CORE TEACHING ARCHITECTURE**

The course should **not** attempt to teach every chapter above during a single short webinar.

The Master TOC is the **entire CAMERA knowledge system**.

The actual paid course will select and sequence the appropriate chapters into **4–6 substantial live educational sessions**, with each session capable of running approximately **60–120 minutes** depending on the final curriculum and audience.

The student receives selected book chapters for **home-study reinforcement**, while the live course concentrates on:

**TEACH → DEMONSTRATE → PRACTICE → CORRECT → IMPLEMENT**

That distinction is important.

The book is the **reference library**.

The slides are the **visual classroom**.

The instructor script is the **teaching voice**.

The workbook is the **student’s implementation workspace**.

The practical exercises are where **knowledge becomes capability**.

And the final project is where the student builds a **working content system for their own business**.

---

# **CAMERA V1 — GOVERNING PRINCIPLE**

**CAMERA does not teach people to become video producers for the sake of producing video.**

It teaches them to determine what communication their business needs, understand how modern media works, and acquire the practical capability to create that communication themselves—using the equipment, software, people, budget, time, and AI tools appropriate to their actual business.

**The objective is not more content.**

**The objective is a business that knows how to use content.**

# **CAMERA V1 — MASTER CURRICULUM & LESSON SPECIFICATION**

**Version:** V1.0  
 **System:** CAMERA™  
 **Purpose:** Complete educational curriculum for teaching businesses and individuals how to produce effective, credible, sustainable modern marketing content themselves.

**Delivery Paths:**

1. **Virtual Cohort — Zoom**  
2. **Live In-Person Workshop / Intensive**

**Primary Role of Instructor:** Teacher, advisor, media trainer and systems instructor — **not production vendor**.

---

# **PART I — CAMERA V1 GOVERNANCE**

## **1.1 What CAMERA Is**

CAMERA is a practical media-production and content-marketing education system.

It teaches participants how to:

* Understand what content their business actually needs.  
* Determine who should be the on-camera authority.  
* Develop effective messages.  
* Present naturally and credibly on camera.  
* Select appropriate cameras, phones, microphones, lighting and support equipment.  
* Configure a usable recording environment.  
* Record professional-looking content without unnecessary complexity.  
* Edit content using an appropriate level of software.  
* Develop platform-appropriate content.  
* Repurpose content efficiently.  
* Incorporate AI and LLM tools into everyday content workflows.  
* Build a sustainable internal content operation.

CAMERA does **not** require a participant to become a professional filmmaker.

The objective is **business-effective content**, not filmmaking for its own sake.

---

# **1.2 What CAMERA Is Not**

CAMERA is not:

* A traditional video-production service.  
* A filmmaking degree.  
* A software certification.  
* A camera-sales program.  
* An influencer-only program.  
* A promise of viral content.  
* A replacement for every outside marketing function.  
* A requirement to purchase expensive equipment.  
* A course teaching participants to imitate one particular creator.

The course teaches principles first and equipment second.

---

# **1.3 CAMERA’s Central Principle**

**The technology serves the message. The message serves the business.**

Participants should never be encouraged to purchase equipment simply because it is more expensive.

Every recommendation must answer:

**What business problem does this solve?**

---

# **1.4 Audience Scope**

CAMERA V1 is intentionally industry-agnostic.

Potential participants include:

* Local businesses  
* Professional services  
* Medical practices  
* Legal practices  
* Automotive businesses  
* Contractors  
* Retail businesses  
* Restaurants  
* Hospitality  
* Real estate  
* Financial services  
* B2B companies  
* Manufacturers  
* Consultants  
* Coaches  
* Founders  
* Executives  
* Sales teams  
* Marketing teams  
* Subject-matter experts  
* Personal brands  
* Public-facing professionals  
* Influencers  
* Spokespeople  
* Businesses with dedicated content personnel

The curriculum teaches a **core system**, then adapts implementation according to the participant’s business.

---

# **1.5 The Three CAMERA Implementation Profiles**

The same CAMERA system may be implemented differently depending on who will create the content.

## **Profile 1 — Business-Owned Marketing**

The business owner or internal team learns to create the company’s marketing content.

Example:

A med spa needs consistent social content.

The course teaches the business how to determine what to create, record it, edit it and publish it without depending on an outside production company for every piece.

---

## **Profile 2 — Permanent Business Authority**

A founder, executive, salesperson, physician, attorney, dealer, spokesperson or other individual becomes the recognizable face of the business.

The course places greater emphasis on:

* On-camera presence  
* Authority  
* Presentation  
* Speaking naturally  
* Repeated content production  
* Personal communication style  
* Content systems

---

## **Profile 3 — Personal Authority / Creator**

The participant’s personal identity is itself central to the business.

Examples include:

* Influencers  
* Experts  
* Coaches  
* Creators  
* Public speakers  
* Personal brands  
* Spokespeople  
* Entertainment-oriented creators

The technical fundamentals remain the same, but the content and platform strategy become more creator-oriented.

---

# **PART II — THE CAMERA FRAMEWORK**

CAMERA remains the organizing framework of the course.

## **C — CHARACTER**

**Who are you on camera?**

Character establishes:

* Professional identity  
* Authority  
* Personality  
* Presence  
* Position  
* Voice  
* Credibility

---

## **A — AUDIENCE**

**Who are you communicating with?**

Audience establishes:

* Who needs the information  
* What they care about  
* What problems they experience  
* What questions they ask  
* What language resonates  
* What causes them to act

---

## **M — MESSAGE / MONETIZATION**

**What are you saying and where does it lead?**

V1 will treat M as the connection between:

* Message  
* Marketing objective  
* Offer  
* Customer journey  
* Call to action  
* Revenue

Content exists for a business reason.

---

## **E — ENGAGEMENT**

**How do we earn and retain attention?**

Engagement includes:

* Hooks  
* Openings  
* Story  
* Pacing  
* Retention  
* Visual variation  
* Pattern interruption  
* Calls to action  
* Conversation

---

## **R — REPURPOSING**

**How does one idea become an efficient content system?**

Repurposing includes:

* Long-form → short-form  
* Video → text  
* Video → email  
* Video → social posts  
* Interviews → multiple assets  
* Recording batches  
* Asset organization  
* Publishing workflow

---

## **A — AI ACCELERATION**

**Where can modern AI make the workflow faster without replacing the human?**

AI is taught as an additional production tool.

It can assist with:

* Research  
* Brainstorming  
* Topic development  
* Outlines  
* Script drafts  
* Hook variations  
* Titles  
* Captions  
* Transcription  
* Repurposing  
* Editing assistance  
* Content calendars  
* Analysis  
* Iteration

The participant remains responsible for:

* Truth  
* Experience  
* Judgment  
* Personality  
* Accuracy  
* Final message  
* Final publication

---

# **PART III — COURSE ARCHITECTURE**

## **CAMERA V1 Core Course**

**Recommended structure:**

**6 instructional units**

Each unit corresponds to one CAMERA dimension.

Each unit contains:

1. Instructor teaching  
2. Client-facing presentation  
3. Demonstration  
4. Practical exercise  
5. Discussion / critique  
6. Implementation assignment  
7. Workbook section  
8. Supporting reference material

---

# **PART IV — UNIT 1**

# **C — CHARACTER**

## **Establishing the Human Behind the Camera**

### **Lesson C1 — What Does It Mean to Be the Face of a Business?**

Teach:

* The difference between a person appearing in a video and becoming a recognizable authority.  
* Why audiences respond differently to people than anonymous corporate messaging.  
* Founder-led versus spokesperson-led communication.  
* Personal credibility versus corporate branding.  
* Authenticity versus performance.

### **Practical Exercise**

Participants identify:

* Who appears on camera?  
* Why that person?  
* What authority do they possess?  
* What should the audience remember about them?

### **Implementation**

Create a:

**Primary On-Camera Authority Profile**

---

# **Lesson C2 — Character vs Persona**

Teach:

* Authentic presentation.  
* Manufactured personality.  
* Professional personality.  
* How to amplify existing strengths.  
* Avoiding imitation of influencers.

### **Exercise**

Complete:

**“What should people feel when I appear on camera?”**

---

# **Lesson C3 — Authority Without Acting**

Teach:

* Confidence  
* Expertise  
* Vulnerability  
* Humor  
* Warmth  
* Directness  
* Credibility  
* Professional restraint

Demonstrate how the same information changes when delivered with different levels of authority.

---

# **Lesson C4 — Finding the Natural Voice**

Teach:

* Conversational language  
* Avoiding corporate speech  
* Avoiding scripted stiffness  
* Speaking to one person  
* Translating expertise into understandable language

### **Exercise**

Explain a complicated business concept:

1. Like a corporate presentation.  
2. Like talking to a customer.  
3. Like explaining it to a friend.

Compare the results.

---

# **Lesson C5 — On-Camera Baseline**

Teach:

* Posture  
* Eye line  
* Facial expression  
* Body position  
* Camera relationship  
* Hand movement  
* Physical presence

### **Practical Assignment**

Record:

**60-Second Introduction**

No production upgrades required.

Phone is acceptable.

---

# **PART V — UNIT 2**

# **A — AUDIENCE**

## **Stop Making Content for Everyone**

### **Lesson A1 — Audience Is Not a Demographic**

Teach:

* Demographics  
* Psychographics  
* Intent  
* Problems  
* Desires  
* Questions  
* Objections

---

# **Lesson A2 — The Person Behind the View**

Teach:

* Who is actually watching?  
* What brought them there?  
* What do they need?  
* What do they fear?  
* What do they want?  
* What do they already believe?

---

# **Lesson A3 — Customer Questions Become Content**

Teach participants to build content from:

* Sales conversations  
* Customer questions  
* Objections  
* Misunderstandings  
* FAQs  
* Complaints  
* Search behavior  
* Internal expertise

### **Exercise**

Create a:

**50-Question Content Bank**

---

# **Lesson A4 — Different Audiences Require Different Content**

Demonstrate how the same business might create different content for:

* New prospect  
* Existing customer  
* High-value customer  
* Skeptical buyer  
* Returning customer  
* Referral source  
* Employee

---

# **Lesson A5 — Audience-to-Topic Mapping**

Participants construct:

**Audience → Problem → Question → Content → Action**

This becomes their first content architecture.

---

# **PART VI — UNIT 3**

# **M — MESSAGE & MONETIZATION**

## **Content Must Have a Job**

### **Lesson M1 — Why Are We Making This Video?**

Teach the difference between:

* Awareness  
* Education  
* Authority  
* Trust  
* Consideration  
* Conversion  
* Retention

---

# **Lesson M2 — Content Pillars**

Participants develop 3–5 primary content categories.

Possible categories:

* Expertise  
* Education  
* Story  
* Demonstration  
* Proof  
* Customer questions  
* Industry commentary  
* Behind the scenes  
* Culture  
* Product/service  
* Objection handling

---

# **Lesson M3 — The Content-to-Business Connection**

Teach:

**Attention → Interest → Trust → Conversation → Transaction**

Not every video needs to sell.

But every business should understand why the video exists.

---

# **Lesson M4 — Calls to Action**

Teach:

* Educational CTA  
* Engagement CTA  
* Lead CTA  
* Consultation CTA  
* Purchase CTA  
* Follow/subscribe CTA

Participants create their:

**CTA Library**

---

# **Lesson M5 — Script Architecture**

Teach:

### **Basic Short-Form Structure**

**HOOK**

↓  
 **PROBLEM**

↓  
 **INSIGHT**

↓  
 **PROOF / EXAMPLE**

↓  
 **ACTION**

### **Long-Form Structure**

**OPEN**

**CONTEXT**

**PROBLEM**

**ARGUMENT**

**EVIDENCE**

**EXAMPLE**

**CONCLUSION**

**NEXT STEP**

---

# **PART VII — UNIT 4**

# **E — ENGAGEMENT**

## **Making the Message Watchable**

### **Lesson E1 — The First Three Seconds**

Teach:

* Why openings matter.  
* Weak openings.  
* Strong openings.  
* Questions.  
* Statements.  
* Contrarian openings.  
* Stories.  
* Demonstrations.

---

# **Lesson E2 — Hook Engineering**

Participants create multiple versions of the same opening.

Exercise:

**One idea → 10 hooks**

---

# **Lesson E3 — Retention**

Teach:

* Information progression  
* Open loops  
* Story tension  
* Visual changes  
* Questions  
* Examples  
* Demonstrations  
* Pacing

---

# **Lesson E4 — On-Camera Delivery**

Teach:

* Pacing  
* Pausing  
* Emphasis  
* Vocal variation  
* Eye contact  
* Energy  
* Conversational delivery  
* Script internalization

---

# **Lesson E5 — Recovering From Mistakes**

Teach:

* Stop and restart.  
* Continue naturally.  
* Correct yourself.  
* Use the mistake.  
* Edit the mistake.  
* Avoid visibly panicking.

Exercise:

**One-Take Recovery Drill**

---

# **PART VIII — UNIT 5**

# **R — REPURPOSING**

## **Stop Creating One Piece of Content at a Time**

### **Lesson R1 — The Content Asset**

Teach the difference between:

**Idea**

**Recording**

**Master Asset**

**Derivative Assets**

**Published Assets**

---

# **Lesson R2 — The 1-to-Many Model**

Example:

One 10-minute authority video can become:

* 1 YouTube video  
* 3–5 short videos  
* 1 LinkedIn post  
* 1 email  
* 3 quote graphics  
* Multiple future topics

The exact ratio is determined by the material rather than an arbitrary formula.

---

# **Lesson R3 — Batch Production**

Teach:

* Topic batching  
* Script batching  
* Recording batching  
* Editing batching  
* Publishing batching

---

# **Lesson R4 — Content Library**

Teach a basic organizational system:

**CONTENT**

→ Ideas  
 → Scripts  
 → Raw  
 → Masters  
 → Shorts  
 → Graphics  
 → Captions  
 → Published  
 → Analytics

---

# **Lesson R5 — The Weekly Content Operating System**

Participants design:

* Recording day  
* Editing time  
* Publishing schedule  
* Review time  
* Content planning time

The objective is a workflow they can actually sustain.

---

# **PART IX — UNIT 6**

# **A — AI ACCELERATION**

## **AI as a Tool Inside the Existing Workflow**

This section is specifically designed for the modern AI market.

The course does **not** teach participants to subscribe to ten AI services.

It teaches:

**Use the minimum number of tools necessary to improve the workflow.**

---

# **Lesson AI1 — What AI Is Actually Good At**

Teach practical applications:

* Brainstorming  
* Research assistance  
* Organization  
* Drafting  
* Summarization  
* Repurposing  
* Editing assistance  
* Analysis  
* Variation generation

---

# **Lesson AI2 — What AI Should Not Replace**

Teach:

* Personal experience  
* Judgment  
* Expertise  
* Personality  
* Truth  
* Original stories  
* Final editorial decisions

---

# **Lesson AI3 — AI-Assisted Scripting**

Demonstrate:

**Human idea**

→ AI outline

→ Human correction

→ AI draft

→ Human rewrite

→ Final script

---

# **Lesson AI4 — Building a Personal Prompt System**

Participants create prompts for:

* Ideas  
* Hooks  
* Scripts  
* Captions  
* Repurposing  
* Research  
* Content calendars

---

# **Lesson AI5 — AI and the Human Voice**

Teach participants to identify:

* Generic AI language  
* Corporate filler  
* Repetitive phrasing  
* False enthusiasm  
* Unnatural transitions

Then teach them how to edit AI output back into their own voice.

---

# **Lesson AI6 — The One-Tool Rule**

Participants identify:

**What do I already have?**

**What is missing?**

**What tool solves that missing problem?**

Only then do they consider adding software.

---

# **PART X — TECHNICAL PRODUCTION SYSTEM**

This component runs throughout the course rather than being isolated from CAMERA.

# **TECH 1 — START WITH THE PHONE**

Teach:

* Phone camera fundamentals  
* Lens selection  
* Resolution  
* Frame rate  
* Orientation  
* Exposure  
* Focus  
* Stability  
* Cleaning the lens

Principle:

A properly configured phone in good light with good audio can produce useful business content.

---

# **TECH 2 — CAMERA CHOICES**

Teach the progression:

**Phone**

→ **Webcam**

→ **Creator camera**

→ **Mirrorless / interchangeable lens**

→ **Multi-camera system**

Participants learn when an upgrade is justified.

---

# **TECH 3 — AUDIO**

Priority:

**Good audio before expensive camera.**

Teach:

* Built-in microphones  
* Wired lavaliers  
* Wireless lavaliers  
* USB microphones  
* XLR microphones  
* Mic placement  
* Room noise  
* Echo  
* Monitoring  
* Recording levels

---

# **TECH 4 — LIGHTING**

Teach:

### **Level 1**

Window light.

### **Level 2**

One LED/key light.

### **Level 3**

Key \+ fill.

### **Level 4**

Key \+ fill \+ background/rim.

Teach:

* Direction  
* Size  
* Distance  
* Diffusion  
* Shadows  
* Exposure  
* Color temperature

---

# **TECH 5 — BACKGROUND**

Teach:

**Background is part of the message.**

Participants learn:

* Depth  
* Separation  
* Distraction removal  
* Professional context  
* Brand cues  
* Books  
* Products  
* Workspace  
* Environment  
* Deliberate visual identity

---

# **TECH 6 — FRAMING & COMPOSITION**

Teach:

* Wide  
* Medium  
* Medium close  
* Close-up  
* Headroom  
* Lead room  
* Eye line  
* Rule of thirds  
* Center framing  
* Symmetry  
* Background depth

---

# **TECH 7 — CAMERA MOVEMENT**

Teach when movement is useful and when it is unnecessary.

Examples:

* Locked-off authority shot  
* Push-in  
* Slider  
* Handheld  
* Walking presentation  
* Demonstration  
* B-roll

---

# **TECH 8 — MULTI-CAMERA**

Teach:

* Primary camera  
* Secondary angle  
* Wide safety  
* Close-up  
* Matching exposure  
* Matching color  
* Cutting between angles

---

# **PART XI — EDITING SYSTEM**

# **EDIT 1 — EDITING PHILOSOPHY**

Teach:

Editing should improve communication, not announce that editing occurred.

---

# **EDIT 2 — BEGINNER WORKFLOW**

Teach using simple software such as:

* CapCut  
* Clipchamp  
* iMovie  
* Equivalent beginner tools

Process:

**Import → Cut → Arrange → Captions → Basic graphics → Audio → Export**

---

# **EDIT 3 — INTERMEDIATE WORKFLOW**

Introduce:

* Descript  
* DaVinci Resolve  
* Other appropriate tools

Only when the participant actually needs additional capability.

---

# **EDIT 4 — TALKING-HEAD EDITING**

Teach:

* Removing mistakes  
* Removing dead air  
* Tightening pacing  
* Jump cuts  
* Captions  
* B-roll  
* Screen inserts  
* Graphics  
* Music

---

# **EDIT 5 — EXPORT**

Teach:

* Resolution  
* Aspect ratio  
* Vertical  
* Horizontal  
* Square  
* Compression  
* File naming  
* Master files  
* Platform exports

---

# **PART XII — PLATFORM APPLICATION**

CAMERA does not require participants to be on every platform.

The question is:

**Where does this business’s audience actually consume information?**

---

# **PLATFORM LESSONS**

## **YouTube**

Teach:

* Search  
* Authority  
* Long-form  
* Shorts  
* Series  
* Titles  
* Thumbnails  
* Retention

---

## **LinkedIn**

Teach:

* Professional authority  
* Founder/executive content  
* Native video  
* Written posts  
* Expertise  
* Business storytelling

---

## **Instagram**

Teach:

* Reels  
* Stories  
* Visual identity  
* Short-form  
* Community

---

## **TikTok**

Teach:

* Short-form communication  
* Hooks  
* Pattern interruption  
* Demonstration  
* Comment-driven content

---

## **Facebook**

Teach:

* Local business communication  
* Community  
* Existing customer relationships  
* Video distribution  
* Groups and pages where relevant

---

# **PART XIII — LIVE PRODUCTION PRACTICUM**

This is where the two delivery paths diverge.

# **VIRTUAL PRACTICUM**

Because students are physically remote, the instructor teaches:

* Setup principles  
* Camera positioning  
* Lighting diagrams  
* Screen demonstrations  
* Student camera audits  
* Student test clips  
* Before/after comparisons  
* Recorded examples

Students perform setup exercises independently.

---

# **IN-PERSON PRACTICUM**

A small demonstration studio/set is established.

Equipment:

* Camera  
* Tripod  
* Monitor/TV  
* Microphone  
* Lights  
* Background  
* Optional second camera

The instructor demonstrates live.

Students can physically see:

**Camera → framing → lighting → monitor → finished image**

This is a major advantage of the in-person version.

---

# **LIVE EXERCISE 1 — CHANGE THE SHOT**

Instructor demonstrates:

* Wide  
* Medium  
* Medium close  
* Close

Participants observe how changing framing changes perceived communication.

---

# **LIVE EXERCISE 2 — CHANGE THE LIGHT**

Demonstrate:

* Front light  
* Side light  
* Overhead light  
* Window light  
* Soft light  
* Hard light

Participants see the difference immediately.

---

# **LIVE EXERCISE 3 — CHANGE THE BACKGROUND**

Demonstrate:

**Accidental background**

versus

**Designed background**

Discuss what each communicates.

---

# **LIVE EXERCISE 4 — AUDIO COMPARISON**

Record:

* Camera microphone  
* Lav  
* USB mic  
* Professional microphone

Discuss the difference.

---

# **LIVE EXERCISE 5 — THE SAME MESSAGE, DIFFERENT DELIVERY**

Instructor demonstrates:

* Reading  
* Memorizing  
* Conversational  
* High energy  
* Calm authority

Participants identify which delivery matches different business contexts.

---

# **PART XIV — VIRTUAL COHORT SPECIFICATION**

## **Recommended Format**

**6 live sessions**

**90–120 minutes each**

Depending on cohort size and exercises.

The virtual course is not a webinar.

It is an actual instructional program.

---

## **Session 1**

**C — Character**

* Course orientation  
* Authority on camera  
* Character vs persona  
* Natural voice  
* Baseline recording  
* Camera presence fundamentals

**Homework:** Character workbook \+ baseline video.

---

## **Session 2**

**A — Audience**

* Audience psychology  
* Questions  
* Problems  
* Content opportunities  
* Audience-to-topic mapping

**Homework:** Audience profile \+ 50-question bank.

---

## **Session 3**

**M — Message & Monetization**

* Content purpose  
* Content pillars  
* Script architecture  
* CTAs  
* Business connection

**Homework:** Content architecture \+ three scripts.

---

## **Session 4**

**E — Engagement \+ Technical Production**

* Hooks  
* Retention  
* Delivery  
* Camera  
* Audio  
* Lighting  
* Background  
* Composition

**Homework:** Produce three videos.

---

## **Session 5**

**R — Repurposing**

* Master asset  
* Derivatives  
* Batch production  
* Editing  
* Content library  
* Workflow

**Homework:** Create one master \+ derivative assets.

---

## **Session 6**

**A — AI Acceleration \+ System Integration**

* AI workflow  
* Prompting  
* Voice  
* AI editing  
* Repurposing  
* Tool selection  
* Final production system  
* 30/60/90-day implementation plan

---

# **PART XV — IN-PERSON VERSION**

The in-person course uses the same intellectual curriculum.

It adds **physical demonstration and supervised implementation.**

## **Recommended Format**

Either:

**One intensive day**

or

**Two half-days**

or

**Three shorter sessions.**

The curriculum does not change.

The delivery mechanism changes.

---

# **IN-PERSON SESSION STRUCTURE**

## **Classroom**

Used for:

* CAMERA concepts  
* Strategy  
* Audience  
* Message  
* AI  
* Platform  
* Workflow

## **Studio/Set**

Used for:

* Camera  
* Lighting  
* Audio  
* Composition  
* Delivery  
* Recording  
* Editing demonstrations

## **Student Practice**

Each participant:

1. Sets up.  
2. Frames.  
3. Lights.  
4. Records.  
5. Reviews.  
6. Receives instruction.  
7. Repeats.

This creates a true **learn → perform → review → correct → perform again** cycle.

# **PART XVII — GEAR REFERENCE SYSTEM**

The final client gear guide will contain four levels.

## **LEVEL 0 — USE WHAT YOU HAVE**

* Existing phone  
* Window  
* Existing computer  
* Free editing software

**Objective:** Start producing.

---

## **LEVEL 1 — SMART STARTER**

Target approximately:

**\$100–\$250**

Potential components:

* Phone tripod  
* Wired/wireless microphone  
* Small LED light  
* Basic accessories

---

## **LEVEL 2 — SERIOUS CREATOR**

Target approximately:

**\$300–\$750**

Potential components:

* Better camera or current-generation phone  
* Better microphone  
* LED/softbox  
* Tripod  
* Basic background  
* Basic accessories

---

## **LEVEL 3 — BUSINESS STUDIO**

Target approximately:

**\$750–\$2,000+**

Potential components:

* Creator/mirrorless camera  
* Quality lens  
* Wireless or professional microphone  
* Key/fill/background lighting  
* Professional support  
* Dedicated background  
* Monitoring

---

## **LEVEL 4 — PROFESSIONAL INTERNAL STUDIO**

For organizations with dedicated personnel.

Potential components:

* Multiple cameras  
* Multiple lenses  
* Professional audio  
* Lighting grid  
* Dedicated set  
* Monitoring  
* Switching  
* Teleprompter  
* Recording/storage infrastructure

**The course teaches participants how to determine whether they actually need this level.**

The course culminates in a practical project.

Each participant produces:

Who represents the business?

### **2\. Audience Profile**

Who is being addressed?

What should the business talk about?

# **PART XXI — THE CAMERA END STATE**

The participant should not finish CAMERA thinking:

“I took a video course.”

They should finish thinking:

**“I now know how my business can produce its own content.”**

They should understand:

**WHO**

should communicate.

**WHO**

they are communicating with.

**WHAT**

they should communicate.

**WHY**

they are communicating it.

**HOW**

to present it.

**HOW**

to record it.

**HOW**

to edit it.

**WHERE**

to publish it.

**HOW**

to reuse it.

**HOW**

AI can accelerate the process.

And most importantly:

**HOW TO KEEP DOING IT WITHOUT NEEDING THE INSTRUCTOR TO PRODUCE IT FOR THEM.**

---

# **PART XXII — V1 BUILD RULE**

The next documents should now be built **from this curriculum specification**, in this order:

**1\. CAMERA V1 Instructor Master Manual**

↓

**2\. CAMERA V1 Student Workbook**

↓

**3\. CAMERA V1 Client Slide Deck**

↓

**4\. CAMERA V1 Instructor Prompter Scripts**

↓

**5\. CAMERA V1 Gear & Software Reference**

↓

**6\. CAMERA V1 Exercises / Assignments / Capstone**

↓

**7\. CAMERA V1 Certificates**

↓

**8\. CAMERA V1 Virtual Cohort Delivery Package**

↓

**9\. CAMERA V1 In-Person Delivery Package**

The critical distinction is that **the slide deck is not the course**.

The slides are the **client-facing visual teaching surface**.

The **Instructor Prompter Script contains the actual 60–120 minutes of teaching, explanation, examples, demonstrations, transitions, questions and exercises** associated with those slides.

That gives us the architecture needed to build the actual course rather than accidentally turning CAMERA into another 20-minute webinar.

**MASTER COURSE ARCHITECTURE / DELIVERY BLUEPRINT**.

But since we already have the **Master TOC** and the **Master Curriculum & Lesson Specification**, we should now stop designing the system and begin building the actual instructional assets.

### **Build order from here**

**1\. CAMERA V1 — MASTER COURSE BOOK**

* Full student-facing instructional text  
* Chapters and subchapters from the approved TOC  
* Concepts explained, not merely listed  
* Practical examples  
* Exercises integrated where appropriate  
* Written as the comprehensive reference students receive for home study  
* This becomes the **source of truth** for the course content

**2\. CAMERA V1 — STUDENT WORKBOOK**

* Exercises  
* Worksheets  
* Assessments  
* Setup inventories  
* Content planning worksheets  
* Camera/on-camera practice logs  
* AI workflow exercises  
* Implementation checklists  
* Course project  
* Final self-assessment

**3\. CAMERA V1 — INSTRUCTOR OPERATING MANUAL**  
 This is the important one you just identified.

It contains, for **every lesson**:

* Lesson objective  
* What the instructor must teach  
* Instructor preparation  
* Required materials  
* Demonstration requirements  
* Live exercises  
* Questions to ask students  
* Expected student responses/results  
* Common problems  
* Instructor correction guidance  
* Timing  
* Zoom adaptation  
* In-person adaptation  
* Homework  
* What students should have completed before the next lesson

This is **not** the student book and not the prompter script.

**4\. CAMERA V1 — SLIDE DECK**  
 Client-facing only.

Each slide supports the instructor’s teaching.

No giant paragraphs. No instructor notes masquerading as slides.

The deck will include:

* Concepts  
* Diagrams  
* Examples  
* Demonstration visuals  
* Before/after comparisons  
* Setup illustrations  
* Frameworks  
* Exercises  
* Key takeaways

**5\. CAMERA V1 — INSTRUCTOR PROMPTER SCRIPTS**  
 Then we write the actual teaching language.

For every slide:

**SLIDE 01 — TITLE**  
 **Instructor Script:**  
 \[What you actually say.\]

Then:

**SLIDE 02 — …**  
 **Instructor Script:**  
 \[What you actually say.\]

The script is designed to support **60–120 minutes of actual teaching**, discussion, demonstrations and exercises—not a 20-minute presentation padded with bullets.

**6\. CAMERA V1 — GEAR & SOFTWARE FIELD GUIDE**  
 A standalone reference containing:

* Phone-first setup  
* Webcam setup  
* Creator setup  
* Professional setup  
* Audio  
* Lighting  
* Cameras  
* Lenses  
* Tripods  
* Teleprompters  
* Backgrounds  
* Room treatment  
* Recording  
* Editing  
* Captions  
* Storage  
* AI tools  
* Free/low-cost paths  
* Upgrade paths  
* What *not* to buy  
* Business/team configurations

This should be maintained separately because gear/software will change faster than the core curriculum.

**7\. CAMERA V1 — COURSE PROJECT**  
 A student takes everything learned and builds their own functioning content-production system.

The project should culminate in something tangible—not simply “complete the workbook.”

**8\. CAMERA V1 — ASSESSMENT & CERTIFICATION SYSTEM**

* Completion requirements  
* Participation requirements  
* Assignments  
* Course-project requirements  
* Instructor assessment  
* Certificate criteria  
* Certificate language  
* Certificate levels, if appropriate

**9\. CAMERA V1 — DELIVERY PACKAGES**  
 Finally we package the exact same intellectual property into:

### **Virtual Cohort**

Zoom-based delivery with the limitations of remote instruction accounted for.

### **In-Person**

Live classroom/stage delivery with the advantages of:

* physical camera  
* monitor/TV  
* live framing demonstration  
* lighting demonstration  
* microphone demonstration  
* students physically moving through setups  
* live on-camera exercises

The **curriculum remains the same**. The delivery method changes.

**2.11 Self-Check — Content vs. Marketing**

For each item below, mark whether it functions primarily as content (demonstrates expertise, builds trust) or marketing (directly promotes an offer).  
   
Explaining a decision or process you use with customers  —  Content ☐   Marketing ☐

Announcing a sale or limited-time offer  —  Content ☐   Marketing ☐

Answering a question customers frequently ask  —  Content ☐   Marketing ☐

Showing a completed project or result  —  Content ☐   Marketing ☐

There is no single correct ratio, but a business made up entirely of the second column rarely builds lasting authority.

**3\. Pick one of those same approaches and describe how you could apply it to your own business in your very next piece of content.**

**PART IV — CHARACTER**

**Workbook Chapter 4 — Character: Who Is On Camera?**

*This workbook chapter is the hands-on companion to Book Chapter 4\. It does not re-explain the concepts of authentic authority or on-camera identity — it gives you a place to identify who will appear on camera for your business and define exactly what that person's on-camera character will be.*  
 

**4.1 Worksheet — Character vs. Persona**

**1\. Describe how you actually talk to a customer or client one-on-one, in person — tone, pace, level of formality.**

 

**2\. Now describe how you imagine you should sound on camera. Circle any word in that answer that does not match your answer to Question 1 — that gap is persona, not character.**

 

**4.2 Self-Assessment — Authentic Authority**

Rate yourself honestly on a scale of 1 (not at all) to 5 (completely) on each statement.  
   
I have real, earned experience in what I would be talking about on camera.   1     2     3     4     5

I could answer a hard question about my field without preparation.   1     2     3     4     5

I feel like an impostor when I imagine presenting myself as an authority.   1     2     3     4     5

I would rather demonstrate my expertise than announce it.   1     2     3     4     5

**4.3–4.8 Worksheet — Identifying Your On-Camera Authority**

Check every role below that realistically describes who could appear on camera for your business.  
   
☐  The Business Owner — I own this business and can speak to it directly.

☐  The Founder — I built this company and its story is part of the message.

☐  A Professional Practitioner — my license, training, or hands-on skill is the authority.

☐  An Employee Spokesperson — someone on staff, not the owner, will represent the business.

☐  Multiple Spokespeople — more than one of the above will appear, in different contexts.

 

**1\. Name the specific person (or people) this applies to.**

 

**2\. If it will be more than one person, describe how their roles on camera will differ from each other.**

 

**4.8 Worksheet — Personal Brand vs. Corporate Brand**

**1\. If you left this business tomorrow, would the on-camera identity you're building leave with you, or stay with the company?**

 

**2\. Is that the outcome you actually want? Explain.**

**PART IV — CHARACTER**

**Workbook Chapter 5 — On-Camera Presence**

*This workbook chapter is the hands-on companion to Book Chapter 5\. It does not re-explain why people look different on camera or what good presence looks like — it gives you a structured way to record yourself, watch it back honestly, and correct specific habits one at a time.*  
 

**5.1 & 5.2 Worksheet — First Recording, No Coaching**

Before reading any further correction advice, record yourself talking for 30 seconds about anything simple (what you had for breakfast, your weekend plans). Do not try to perform. Then answer honestly.  
 

**1\. What surprised you when you watched it back?**

 

**2\. Did you feel like “yourself” on screen? If not, describe the gap.**

 

Keep this recording. You will compare it to a later recording at the end of this chapter.

**5.3–5.9 Self-Assessment — Physical Fundamentals**

Watch your recording from Section 5.1 again. This time, check the box only if it was a genuine problem in that specific clip.  
   
☐  Eye Line — my eyes drifted away from the lens or wandered.

☐  Posture — I was slouched, leaning, or unevenly balanced.

☐  Body Position — I was angled awkwardly or too far off-center.

☐  Facial Expression — my face looked flat, tense, or mismatched to what I was saying.

☐  Hand Movement — my hands were distracting, stiff, or completely absent from frame.

☐  Natural Movement — I was either frozen still or fidgeting.

☐  Stillness — I could not hold a steady, grounded position between movements.

   
Pick the ONE item above that was the most noticeable problem. That is your priority fix for this chapter.  
   
Priority fix: \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_

**5.10–5.14 Self-Assessment — Presence Qualities**

Rate your first recording (Section 5.1) honestly on a scale of 1 (not at all present) to 5 (fully present) for each quality.  
   
Energy — did I sound alive, or flat?   1     2     3     4     5

Confidence — did I sound sure of what I was saying?   1     2     3     4     5

Authority — did I sound like someone worth listening to?   1     2     3     4     5

Warmth — did I sound approachable, not cold or robotic?   1     2     3     4     5

Conversational Presence — did it sound like I was talking WITH someone, not AT them?   1     2     3     4     5

   
Whichever quality scored lowest, describe one small, specific change that might raise it (example: smiling before you start speaking, imagining a specific real person you're talking to):  
 

**5.15 Worksheet — Avoiding “Talking Head” Syndrome**

**1\. In your first recording, did you move, gesture, or change energy at all during the 30 seconds, or did you stay in one fixed mode the whole time?**

 

**2\. Name one real moment from your own life or work where you naturally got animated telling a story. What made that different from how you were on camera?**

 

 

**5.16 & 5.17 Exercise — Looking Into the Lens vs. Looking at a Screen**

Record two more 15-second clips: one looking directly into the camera lens, one looking at your own image on the screen while talking.  
 

**1\. Watch both back. Which one feels more like you are speaking TO the viewer, rather than AT or near them?**

 

**2\. Which one was more physically comfortable for you to sustain?**

 

If the two answers conflict, note that here — it is common, and later chapters on equipment setup will help resolve it.  
 

**5.18–5.22 Setup Worksheet — Camera Height, Distance, and Framing**

Set up your camera as you currently do for recording, then check each item below against your own frame.  
   
☐  Camera Height — the lens is at or near my eye level, not looking up my nose or down at me.

☐  Camera Distance — I am close enough to feel present, not shrunk into the middle of the frame.

☐  Framing — my head and shoulders are positioned according to standard framing conventions, not off-center by accident.

☐  Headroom — there is a reasonable, not excessive, gap between the top of my head and the top of the frame.

☐  Background Relationship — my background supports my message rather than distracting from it.

   
List anything you unchecked above, and what you will physically change before your next recording:

**1\. Watch both recordings back to back. What specifically improved?**

 

 

**2\. What still needs work, honestly?**

 

**3\. Show the second recording to one other person. Ask them what they noticed, without prompting them toward any particular answer. Write their response below.**

**PART V — A: AUDIENCE**

**Workbook Chapter 6 — Understanding the Person Watching**

*This workbook chapter is the hands-on companion to Book Chapter 6\. It does not re-explain the concepts of audience and intent — it gives you a place to build a real, specific profile of the person your content needs to reach.*  
 

**6.1 & 6.2 Worksheet — Beyond Demographics**

Resist the urge to answer with age ranges or income brackets. Answer these in plain, specific language, as if describing one real person you know.  
 

**1\. Picture the single best customer or client you have ever had. Without naming them, describe who they are and what they needed.**

 

 

**2\. What was going on in their life or business right before they came to you?**

 

**6.3 Worksheet — Buyer vs. Viewer**

**1\. Will the person watching your content usually be the same person who pays you? If not, describe the difference.**
